The formal theory of tangentads PART II
MARCELLO LANFRANCHI

Abstract
Tangent category theory is a well-established categorical framework for differential geometry. A long list of fundamental geometric constructions, such as the tangent bundle functor, vector fields, Euclidean spaces, and vector bundles have been successfully generalized and internalized within tangent categories. Over the past decade, the theory has also been extended in several directions, yielding concepts such as tangent monads, tangent fibrations, tangent restriction categories, and reverse tangent categories. It is natural to wonder how these new flavours of the theory interact with the geometric constructions. How does a tangent monad or a tangent fibration lift to the tangent category of differential bundles of a tangent category? What is the correct notion of connections for a tangent restriction category? In previous work, we introduced tangentads, a unifying framework that generalizes many tangent-like notions, and developed a formal theory of vector fields for tangentads. In this paper, we extend this formal theory to three further fundamental constructions. These are differential objects, which generalize Euclidean spaces, differential bundles, which represent vector bundles in tangent category theory, and connections on differential bundles, which are the analogue of Koszul connections. These notions are introduced in the general theory of tangentads via appropriate universal properties. We then extend some of the main results of tangent category theory, including the equivalence between differential objects and differential bundles over the terminal object, and show that connections admit well-defined notions of covariant derivative, curvature, and torsion. Finally, we construct connections using PIE limits and apply our framework to several concrete instances of tangentads.



1
Introduction

In recent years, tangent category theory, a well-established categorical framework for differential geometry, has been extended in new directions. In
[
5
]
, the notion of tangent fibrations was introduced to study differential bundles. Tangent monads were studied in
[
12
]
as the tangent-categorical analogue of monads, while tangent restriction categories were introduced in
[
3
]
to take into account the partiality of morphisms.

It is natural to ask whether these new contexts admit extensions of the constructions and results of tangent category theory. Furthermore, one might hope to develop such extensions in a way that not only encompasses the existing flavours of tangent category theory, but can also be applied in future investigations in other settings.

In previous papers
[
15
]
, we developed a formal theory of vector fields by generalizing several results from the theory of vector fields to tangent fibrations, tangent monads, and tangent restriction categories. Our approach was, in fact, more general. We introduced a notion of vector fields for any tangentad, which is a
2
-categorical formalization of a tangent category. We proved that vector fields in this general context carry both a Lie algebra structure and a
2
-monad structure. We also showed how to construct vector fields in the presence of PIE limits, which are a special class of
2
-limits.

In this paper, we develop a formal theory for three other important constructions of tangent category theory, that is differential objects, differential bundles, and connections on differential bundles. Differential objects are the analogue in tangent categories of Euclidean spaces, that is, spaces whose tangent bundle is isomorphic to two copies of the space itself. Differential bundles are the analogue of vector bundles in differential geometry, and connections on differential bundles correspond to Koszul connections on vector bundles.

We begin by recalling the definition of tangentads, introduced in previous work
[
13
]
, which provide an abstraction of tangent categories in the general context of
2
-category theory. In particular, tangent fibrations, tangent monads, and tangent restriction categories are all examples of tangentads in suitable
2
-categories. We also recall the definition of Cartesian tangentads, which are Cartesian objects in the
2
-category of tangentads.

After briefly recalling the main definitions and results concerning differential objects in the context of tangent categories, we present the universal property satisfied by this construction and use it to characterize differential objects for arbitrary Cartesian tangentads. We prove that this construction is
2
-functorial and compute it for several examples of tangentads.

We then carry out analogous developments for differential bundles and for connections: we recall the main definitions and results in the context of tangent categories; we present the universal properties enjoyed by these constructions, and extend to the formal context some results. We prove that, under mild conditions, a tangentad admits the construction of differential objects whenever it admits the construction of differential bundles, generalizing the important theorem that establishes that differential bundles over the terminal object are equivalent to differential objects. We construct a notion of curvature and torsion for connections, and construct connections using PIE limits.

acknowledgements
.
The author would like to thank JS Lemay, Richard Garner, and Steve Lack for useful discussions and suggestions on this work. This material is based upon work supported by the AFOSR under award number FA9550-24-1-0008.

2
Recollection on tangentads

Tangentads were initially introduced in
[
14
]
with the name of tangent objects, to establish a Grothendieck construction in the context of tangent categories. In
[
13
]
, this notion was revisited and proved to be suitable to capture a long list of existing concepts in tangent category theory, such as tangent fibrations, tangent monads, tangent split restriction categories, reverse tangent categories, strong display tangent categories, and infinitesimal objects. In this section, we briefly review the definition of a tangentad in a
2
-category.

2.1
The definition of a tangentad

The notion of tangentads was inspired by Leung’s approach to tangent categories
[
16
]
. In this section, we recall this definition in a more explicit form.

(
[

)
.

A tangentad in a

consists of the following data:

F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}

Negation
]
:

A

such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle n,\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

We suggest the reader consult
[
13
]
for more details on this definition and for more examples.

Example 2.2
.

Tangent categories, initially introduced in
[
18
]
, are the archetypal example of tangentads. In particular, a tangent category is precisely a tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories.

Example 2.3
.

Tangent monads, introduced in
[
12
, Definition 19]
, are monads in the
2
-category of tangent categories. Concretely, a tangent monad on a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
consists of a monad
S\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
equipped with a natural transformation
\alpha_{M}\colon S\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}SM
, for
M\in\mathbb{X}
, such that
(S,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a lax tangent morphism. Furthermore, the unit
\eta_{M}\colon M\to SM
and the multiplication
\mu_{M}\colon S^{2}M\to SM
of the monad
S
are tangent natural transformations, that is, they are compatible with
\alpha
.

More generally, a tangent monad internal to a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
is monad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

[
13
, Proposition 4.2]
shows that, for a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
, the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))
of monads in the
2
-category of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
is isomorphic to the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{MND}({\mathbf{K}}))
of tangentads in the
2
-category of monads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. In particular, tangent monads are tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{MND}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathsf{MND}(\mathsf{CAT})
of monads.

Example 2.4
.

Tangent fibrations, introduced in
[
5
, Definition 5.2]
, are (cloven) fibrations
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between two tangent categories which preserve the tangent structures strictly, that is,
\Pi
strictly preserves the tangent structures, and the tangent bundle functors define a Cartesian morphism of fibrations.

[
14
, Proposition 5.1]
proves that tangent fibrations are tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
. Concretely, the objects of
\mathsf{FIB}
are triples
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime};\Pi)
formed by two categories
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and a fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
.
1
-morphisms of
\mathsf{FIB}

(F,F^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime};\Pi_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\bullet};\Pi_{\bullet})
are pairs of functors
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
and
F^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime}
which strictly commute with the fibrations, that is,
\Pi_{\bullet}\circ F^{\prime}=F\circ\Pi_{\circ}
, and preserve the Cartesian lifts, that is,
F^{\prime}
sends each Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
of a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
to a Cartesian lift of
Ff
. Finally,
2
-morphisms of
\mathsf{FIB}

\displaystyle(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime})\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathbb{X}_{\circ}^{\prime};\Pi_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime};\Pi_{\bullet})

are pairs of natural transformations
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}\Rightarrow G^{\prime}
which commute with the fibrations, that is,
\Pi_{\bullet}\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi_{\Pi_{\circ}}
.

Example 2.5
.

Tangent indexed categories were introduced in
[
14
, Definition 5.3]
as tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{INDX}
of indexed categories. Concretely, the objects of
\mathsf{INDX}
are pairs
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{I})
formed by a category
\mathbb{X}
and an indexed category, a.k.a., a pseudofunctor
\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
. The
1
-morphisms
(F,F^{\prime},\xi)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathscr{I}_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathscr{I}_{\bullet})
of
\mathsf{INDX}
are pseudonatural tranformations, that are, functors
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\circ}\to\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}
together with a collection of functors
F_{A}^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{I}_{\circ}(A)\to\mathscr{I}_{\bullet}(FA)
, indexed by the objects
A
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
, and a collection of natural isomorphisms
\xi^{f}\colon F_{A}^{\prime}\circ\mathscr{I}_{\circ}(f)\Rightarrow\mathscr{I}_{\bullet}(Ff)\circ F_{B}^{\prime}
, called distributors, indexed by the morphisms
f\colon A\to B
of
\mathbb{X}_{\circ}
. Finally, the
2
-morphisms of
\mathsf{INDX}

\displaystyle(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime},\xi)\to(G,G^{\prime},\kappa)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\circ},\mathscr{I}_{\circ})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathscr{I}_{\bullet})

consist of a natural transformation
\varphi\colon F\Rightarrow G
together with a collection
\varphi^{\prime}
of natural transformations
\varphi_{A}^{\prime}\colon F_{A}^{\prime}\Rightarrow G_{A}^{\prime}\circ\mathscr{I}_{\bullet}(\varphi_{A})
which are compatible with the distributors.

A tangent indexed category, that is, a tangentad in
\mathsf{INDX}
, consists of the following data:

​
[
Base tangent category
]
:

A tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
;

​
[
Indexed category
]
:

An indexed category
\mathscr{I}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
which sends each object
M\in\mathbb{X}
to a category
\mathbb{X}^{M}
and each morphism
f\colon M\to N
to a functor
f^{\ast}\mathbb{X}^{N}\to\mathbb{X}^{N}
;

​
[
Indexed tangent functor
]
:

A list of functors
\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}\colon\mathbb{X}^{M}\to\mathbb{X}^{\mathrm{T}M}
, indexed by the objects of
M
, together with a list of natural isomorphisms
\xi^{f}_{E}\colon(\mathrm{T}f)^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime N}E\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}f^{\ast}E
, indexed by the morphisms
f\colon M\to N
of
\mathbb{X}
;

​
[
Indexed projection
]
:

A list of natural transformations
p^{\prime M}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\to p^{\ast}E
;

​
[
Indexed zero morphism
]
:

A list of natural transformations
z^{\prime M}_{E}\colon E\to z^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E
;

​
[
Indexed sum morphism
]
:

A list of natural transformations
s^{\prime M}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}_{2}E\to s^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E
;

​
[
Indexed vertical lift
]
:

A list of natural transformations
l^{\prime M}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\to l^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E
;

​
[
Indexed canonical flip
]
:

A list of natural transformations
c^{\prime M}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E\to c^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{\prime\mathrm{T}M}\mathrm{T}^{\prime M}E
.

The indexed projection, zero morphism, sum morphism, vertical lift, and canonical lift must also be compatible with the distributors of
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
and need to satisfy the axioms of a tangent structure.

Finally, we mention that
[
14
, Theorem 5.5]
shows a
2
-equivalence between the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGFIB}
of tangent fibrations and the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGINDX}
of tangent indexed categories.

Example 2.6
.

Tangent restriction categories, introduced in
[
3
, Definition 6.14]
, are restriction categories equipped with an endofunctor
\mathrm{T}
which preserves the restriction idempotents, together with structural total natural transformations
p_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to M
,
z_{M}\colon M\to\mathrm{T}M
,
s_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}M\to\mathrm{T}M
,
l_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
, and
c_{M}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}M\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
similar to the structural natural transformations of a tangent structure and that satisfy similar axioms, but in which the
n
-fold pullback of
p
along itself and the pullback diagram of the universality of the vertical lift are replaced with restriction pullbacks. We suggest the reader consult
[
8
]
for a definition of restriction category and
[
9
]
for a definition of restriction limits. As noticed in
[
13
, Section 4.6]
, tangent restriction categories are not an example of tangentads. Nevertheless, as proved in
[
13
, Proposition 4.35]
, tangent split restriction categories, that is, tangent restriction categories whose restriction idempotents split, are precisely the tangentads in the
2
-category
\mathsf{SRCAT}
of split restriction categories, restriction functors, and total natural transformations.

As proved by
[
13
, Lemma 4.36]
, every tangent restriction category embeds into a tangent split restriction category. Thus, in particular, every tangent restriction category embeds into a tangentad.

2.2
The pointwise tangent structure on the Hom categories

In this section, we recall a construction presented in
[
15
]
. We show that the Hom-categories in the
2
-category of tangentads of a given
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
come with a tangent structure. This plays a crucial role in our story.

Proposition 2.7
.

For two tangentads
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
(with negatives), the category of lax tangent morphisms
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
from
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
to
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
comes with a tangent structure (with negatives) defined pointwise. Concretely, the tangent bundle functor sends a lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to the lax tangent morphism:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{(F,\alpha)}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{T},c)}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Moreover,
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sends a morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\to(G,\beta)
of lax tangent morphisms to the morphism:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathrm{T}\varphi\colon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(F,\alpha)\to\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)

Furthermore, the Hom-category
2
-functor
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})(-,?)
lifts along the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{TNGCAT}\to\mathsf{CAT}
to a
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})\to\mathsf{TNGCAT}

strict and contravariant in the first argument and lax and covariant in the second.

In the following, we denote by
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
the Hom-tangent category of two tangentads
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of a given
2
-category.

2.3
Cartesian tangentads

In
[
13
, Section 3.2]
, Cartesian tangentads were introduced. In this section, we recall this definition. In the following, we assume that
{\mathbf{K}}
is a Cartesian
2
-category, that is, a
2
-category with finite
2
-products and we denote by
\mathds{1}
the
2
-terminal object of
{\mathbf{K}}
and by
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
the Cartesian
2
-product of two objects
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Definition 2.8

(
[
7
, Section 5]
)
.

In a Cartesian
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
, a
Cartesian object
consists of an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with two
1
-morphisms

\displaystyle\ast\colon\mathds{1}\to\mathbb{X}

\displaystyle\times\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}

which are right adjoints to the terminal
1
-morphism
!\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathds{1}
and to the diagonal map
\Delta\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
, respectively.

Cartesian objects in the
2
-category of categories are precisely Cartesian categories, that is, categories with finite products. Cartesian objects in a
2
-category form a new
2
-category denoted by
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
whose
1
-morphisms are
1
-morphisms of
{\mathbf{K}}
which preserve the Cartesian structure up to
2
-isomorphisms, and whose
2
-morphisms are
2
-morphisms of
{\mathbf{K}}
which commute with the coherence
2
-isomorphisms of the
1
-morphisms. Furthermore, if
{\mathbf{K}}
is a Cartesian
2
-category so does the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. We suggest the reader consult
[
13
]
for details.

Definition 2.9

(
[
13
, Definition 3.18]
)
.

A
Cartesian tangentad
is equivalently a tangentad in the
2
-category
\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}})
of Cartesian objects of
{\mathbf{K}}
or a Cartesian object in the Cartesian
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{CTNG}({\mathbf{K}})
the
2
-category
\mathsf{CART}(\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}}))\cong\mathsf{TNG}(\mathsf{CART}({\mathbf{K}}))
of Cartesian tangentads. Concretely, a Cartesian tangentad consists of a Cartesian object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
equipped with a tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
in
{\mathbf{K}}
whose tangent bundle
1
-morphism
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
such that the
2
-morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\ast}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\times\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}

induced by the adjunctions
\ast\dashv!
and
\times\dashv\Delta
are invertible and compatible with the structural
2
-morphisms of the tangent structure.

2.4
The Cartesian structure of the Hom-tangent categories

In this section, we show that the Hom-tangent categories inherit a Cartesian structure provided the target tangentad is Cartesian. Consider an ambient
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}
which admits finite
2
-products, whose terminal object is denoted by
\mathds{1}
and Cartesian
2
-product by
\times
. A Cartesian object of
{\mathbf{K}}
is an object
\mathbb{X}
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with two
1
-morphisms

\displaystyle\ast\colon\mathds{1}\to\mathbb{X}

\displaystyle\times\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}

which are right adjoints to the
1
-morphisms
!\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathds{1}
and
\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\langle\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}},\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\rangle\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
, respectively. The next result establishes that the Hom-categories
{\mathbf{K}}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{X}]
are Cartesian provided that
\mathbb{X}
is a Cartesian object of
{\mathbf{K}}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

If
\mathbb{X}
is a Cartesian object of
{\mathbf{K}}
, the Hom-categories
{\mathbf{K}}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{X}]
are Cartesian, whose terminal object is the
1
-morphism

\displaystyle\top\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{!^{\prime}}\mathds{1}\xrightarrow{\ast}\mathbb{X}

and whose Cartesian product between two
1
-morphisms
A,B\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
is the
1
-morphism

\displaystyle A\Box B\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\langle A,B\rangle}\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\xrightarrow{\times}\mathbb{X}

and whose projections are the
2
-morphisms

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\colon A\times B=\times\circ\langle A,B\rangle\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}\langle A,B\rangle}\Pi_{1}\circ\langle A,B\rangle=A

\displaystyle\pi_{2}\colon A\times B=\times\circ\langle A,B\rangle\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}\langle A,B\rangle}\Pi_{2}\circ\langle A,B\rangle=B

where
\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
are the two projections and
\pi_{k}\colon\times\Rightarrow\Pi_{k}
are the corresponding
2
-morphisms.

Proof.

Consider the units and the counits of the adjunctions
(\eta^{\ast},\varepsilon^{\ast})\colon!\dashv\ast
and
(\eta^{\times},\varepsilon^{\times})\colon\Delta\dashv\times
in
{\mathbf{K}}
:

\displaystyle\eta^{\ast}

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\to\ast\>\circ\>!

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\ast}

\displaystyle\colon!\circ\ast\to\mathsf{id}_{\mathds{1}}

\displaystyle\eta^{\times}

\displaystyle\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\to\times\circ\Delta

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\times}

\displaystyle\colon\Delta\circ\times\to\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}}

Consider a
1
-morphism
A\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
and define the following
2
-morphism

\displaystyle A\xrightarrow{\eta^{\ast}_{A}}\ast\>\circ\>!\circ A=\ast\>\circ\>!^{\prime}

where we used that
!\circ A=!^{\prime}
, since
\mathds{1}
is
2
-terminal in
{\mathbf{K}}
. If
\varphi\colon A\to\top
is a
2
-morphism, by employing the counit
\varepsilon^{\ast}
we define a
2
-morphism

\displaystyle!\circ A\xrightarrow{\varphi}!\circ\ast\>\circ\>!\circ A\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{\ast}!A}!\circ A

However, since
\mathds{1}
is
2
-terminal in
{\mathbf{K}}
, such a morphism must coincide with the identity on
!\circ A
. Therefore, by employing the unit again and using the triangle identities, we conclude that
\varphi
must coincide with
\eta^{\ast}
, that is, that
\top
is the terminal object of
{\mathbf{K}}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{X}]
. To prove that
A\Box B
is the Cartesian product of two
1
-morphisms
A,B\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
, first, notice that the counit
\varepsilon^{\times}
gives rise to the two projections

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\colon\times=\Pi_{1}\circ\Delta\circ\times\xrightarrow{\Pi_{1}(\varepsilon^{\times})}\Pi_{1}

\displaystyle\pi_{2}\colon\times=\Pi_{2}\circ\Delta\circ\times\xrightarrow{\Pi_{2}(\varepsilon^{\times})}\Pi_{2}

where we used that
\Pi_{k}\circ\Delta=\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
since
\Delta=\langle\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}},\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\rangle
, by definition. From this and using the triangle identities of
\eta^{\times}
and
\varepsilon^{\times}
, one deduces that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\Box B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

is a product diagram in
{\mathbf{K}}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{X}]
.
∎

[
13
, Definition 3.13]
establishes that a Cartesian tangentad is a Cartesian object in the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. From this observation, it follows by Lemma
2.10
that the Hom-categories
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
are Cartesian categories, provided that
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a Cartesian tangentad. One also needs the tangent structure of Proposition
2.7
to be compatible with the Cartesian structure.

Proposition 2.11
.

The Hom-tangent categories
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
are Cartesian tangent categories provided that
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a Cartesian tangentad.

Proof.

Since the tangent structure and the Cartesian structure of
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
are both induced by the tangent structure and the Cartesian structure of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, which are compatible since it is a Cartesian object in
\mathsf{TNG}({\mathbf{K}})
, it follows that
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
is a Cartesian tangent category.
∎

3
The formal theory of differential objects

In every Cartesian tangent category, one can define a suitable class of objects, called
differential objects
, that generalize Euclidean spaces in differential geometry. This section aims to formalize this notion in the context of tangentads.

3.1
Differential objects in tangent category theory

We start by recalling the definition of a differential object.

Definition 3.1

(
[
3
, Definition 4.8]
)
.

A
differential object
in a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is a four-tuple
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
consisting of an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
and three morphisms, so defined:

​
[
Zero morphism
]
:

A morphism
\zeta_{A}\colon\ast\to A
;

​
[
Sum morphism
]
:

A morphism
\sigma_{A}\colon A\times A\to A
;

such that the triple
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
is a commutative monoid in the Cartesian category
\mathbb{X}
. Moreover:

​
[
Differential projection
]
:

A morphism
{\hat{p}}_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A
;

which satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

Additivity 1. The morphism
{\hat{p}}_{A}\colon(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A})\to(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
is a morphism of commutative monoids, where we identify
\mathrm{T}(A\times A)
with
\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A
and
\mathrm{T}\ast
with
\ast
. Concretely, this corresponds to the commutativity of the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}\times{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

(b)

Additivity 2. The morphism
(!,{\hat{p}}_{A})\colon(p\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A,z_{A},s_{A})\to(!\colon A\to\ast,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
is an additive bundle morphism. Concretely, this corresponds to the commutativity of the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}\times_{!}{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

(c)

Linearity. The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

(d)

Universality. The following is a product diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{p}}_{A}}

The commutative monoid structure of a differential object captures the additive structure of the vector space underlying a Euclidean space, where
\zeta_{A}\colon\ast\to A
sets the zero vector and
\sigma_{A}\colon A\times A\to A
represents the sum of vectors. The differential projection
{\hat{p}}_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A
corresponds to the canonical map which identifies the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{x}A
at each point
x
of a Euclidean space
A
to the space
A
itself, by sending a tangent vector
v
of
\mathrm{T}_{x}A
to the point
x+v
, where
x
is intepreted both as a point of
A
and as a vector.

In a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
there is a Cartesian tangent category, denoted by
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, whose objects are differential objcts
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
and morphisms are linear morphisms of differential objects, that are, morphisms
f\colon A\to B
that commute with the differential projection,
\hat{p}_{A}\circ f=\mathrm{T}f\circ\hat{p}_{B}
. The tangent bundle functor of
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
sends a differential object
(A,\zeta_{,}\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
to the differential object
(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{\mathrm{T}A})
, where we identified
\mathrm{T}(A\times A)
with
\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A
and
\mathrm{T}\ast
with
\ast
, and whose differential projection is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\hat{p}}_{\mathrm{T}A}\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}A\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{T}^{2}A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}{\hat{p}}_{A}}\mathrm{T}A

Finally, the structural natural transformations of
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
are defined as in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
3
, Section 4.1]
)
.

There is a
2
-endofunctor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}\colon\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}

on the category of Cartesian tangent categories and strong tangent morphisms which preserve finite products, that sends a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to the Cartesian tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and that sends a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which preserves finite products to the strong tangent morphism which sends a differential object
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{A})
to the differential object
(FA,F\zeta_{A},F\sigma_{A},{\hat{p}}_{FA})
, whose differential projection is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\hat{p}}_{FA}\colon\mathrm{T}FA\xrightarrow{\alpha^{-1}}F\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A\xrightarrow{F{\hat{p}}_{A}}FA

3.2
The universal property of differential objects

This section aims to characterize the correct universal property of the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects and linear morphisms of a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. For starters, consider the forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which sends a differential object
A
to its underlying object of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Since
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is Cartesian, there are two (strong) tangent morphisms

\displaystyle\ast

\displaystyle\colon\mathds{1}\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\times(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

sending the unique object of the terminal tangent category
\mathds{1}
to the (chosen) terminal object
\ast
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and sending a pair of objects
(A,B)
to their Cartesian product
A\times B
, respectively. Therefore, we can define the following natural transformations

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}\colon\ast(!(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A}))=\ast\xrightarrow{\zeta_{A}}A=\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}\colon\times(\langle\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}\rangle(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A}))=A\times A\xrightarrow{\sigma_{A}}A=\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})

where
!\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\ast
is the terminal morphism, that is, the constant functor. However, as shown by Lemma
2.10
and by Proposition
2.11
, the functors
\ast\>\circ\>!
and
\times\circ\langle\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}\rangle
are precisely the terminal object
\top
and the Cartesian product
\mathsf{U}\Box\mathsf{U}
in the Cartesian Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with the natural transformations
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
, is a commutative monoid in the Cartesian Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Proof.

First, we need to prove that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
are tangent natural transformations. Thanks to
[
5
, Propositions 3.7 and 2.16]
, linear morphisms of differential objects are additive, that is they preserve the commutative monoid structures. In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
are natural transformations. To prove they are compatible with the tangent structure, notice that, by definition of the tangent structure on
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}=\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\mathcal{Z}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})})

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}=\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A}=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\mathcal{S}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})})

Thus,
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{S}
are compatible with the tangent structure. To prove that
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
defines a commutative monoid, one simply uses the fact that each
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A})
constitutes a commutative monoid.
∎

The next step is to show that the commutative monoid
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
comes with a differential projection. Define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}\colon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})=\mathrm{T}A\xrightarrow{\hat{p}_{A}}A=\mathsf{U}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})

By the linearity of the morphisms in
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
,
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
is natural. Furthermore, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}=\mathrm{T}\hat{p}_{A}\circ c

and
c^{2}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\circ\mathsf{U}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\circ\mathsf{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2}\circ\mathsf{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{U}\circ\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}\circ\mathsf{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathsf{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mathcal{\hat{P}}}

Thus,
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
is a tangent natural transformation.

Definition 3.4
.

A
pointwise differential object
consists of a differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in a Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
such that, the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(G,\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mup\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}}

is a pointwise product diagram in
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The forgetful functor
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with the tangent natural transformations
\mathcal{Z}
,
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
defines a pointwise differential object in the Cartesian Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
.

Proof.

In Lemma
3.3
we proved that
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
is a commutative monoid in
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. The equational axioms required for
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
to be a differential projection of
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S})
, are a consequence of
\mathcal{\hat{P}}_{(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}
being the differential projection of each differential object
(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. Notice that the compatibility with the vertical lift is also a consequence of the compatibility between
l_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}A\to\mathrm{T}^{2}A
and
\hat{p}_{A}
. Finally, to prove the universal property of
\mathcal{\hat{P}}
, notice that limits on the target tangent category induce pointwise limits in the Hom-tangent category.
∎

To characterize the correct universal property enjoyed by the pointwise differential object of Proposition
3.5
, for starters, let us unwrap the definition of a differential object in each Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]
, where
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
are Cartesian tangent categories:

​
[
Base object
]
:

The base object is a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
;

​
[
Zero morphism
]
:

The zero morphism consists of a tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\zeta\colon\top\to(G,\beta)

​
[
Sum morphism
]
:

The sum morphism consists of a tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\sigma\colon(G,\beta)\>\Box\>(G,\beta)\to(G,\beta)

​
[
Differential projection
]
:

The differential projection consists of a tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\hat{p}\colon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)\to(G,\beta)

satisfying the axioms of a pointwise differential object. A morphism of differential objects in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]
from a pointwise differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
to another one
(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime};\zeta^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\hat{p}^{\prime})
consists of a tangent natural transformation
\varphi\colon(G,\beta)\to(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
which commutes with the differential structures.

For each tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, a pointwise differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
induces a functor

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]

which sends a lax tangent morphism
(H,\gamma)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
to the pointwise differential object

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=((G,\beta)\circ(H,\gamma);\zeta_{(H,\gamma)},\sigma_{(H,\gamma)},\hat{p}_{(H,\gamma)})

in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
. Concretely, the base object of
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)
is the lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{(H,\gamma)}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{(G,\beta)}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})

while the zero, the sum, and the differential projection of
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)
are the natural transformations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)\Box(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(G,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}}

As observed for vector fields
[
15
, Section 3]
, this construction is functorial and extends to a tangent morphism.

Lemma 3.6
.

A differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
induces a lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]

for each tangentad
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. Furthermore,
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}
is natural in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and it is strong (strict) when
(G,\beta)
is strong (strict).

Proof.

To define the induced lax tangent morphism
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}
, one can readily extend the argument used in the discussion above for tangent categories by replacing the natural transformations with
2
-morphisms of
{\mathbf{K}}
. We leave the details to the reader. Furthermore, the distributive law
\beta
induces a distributive law on
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}
such that when
(G,\beta)
is strong, respectively strict, so is
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}
. To prove that
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
is natural in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, consider two lax tangent morphisms
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(H,\gamma)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime})
. Thus:

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}\circ[F,\alpha\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}])(H,\gamma))=\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}((H,\gamma)\circ(F,\alpha))

\displaystyle\quad=\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}((H,\gamma))\circ(F,\alpha)=\mathsf{DO}[F,\alpha\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}]\circ\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}(H,\gamma)

Finally, using that each
[F,\alpha\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}]
is a strict tangent morphism,
\Gamma_{(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})}
becomes a tangent natural transformation.
∎

In particular, by Lemma
3.6
, the differential object
(\mathsf{U};\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
of Proposition
3.5
induces a strict tangent natural transformation

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]

natural in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
.

We want to prove that
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
is invertible. Consider another tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
together with a differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. For every
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, the tuple

\displaystyle\left(GA,\zeta_{A}^{\prime}\colon\ast\to GA,\sigma_{A}^{\prime}\colon GA\times GA\to GA,\hat{p}_{A}^{\prime}\colon GA\to\mathrm{T}GA\right)

is a differential object in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Therefore, we can define a functor

\displaystyle\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential object
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
, and a morphism
f\colon A\to B
to
Gf
. By the naturality of
\zeta
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{p}
,

\displaystyle Gf\colon(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})\to(GB,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})

becomes a linear morphism of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Furthermore,
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
comes with a distributive law

\displaystyle\Lambda[\beta]\colon\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}](\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A)=(G\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A,\zeta_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A},\sigma_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A},\hat{p}_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}A})\xrightarrow{\beta_{A}}(\mathrm{T}GA,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A}^{\prime},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A},c_{A}\circ\mathrm{T}\hat{p}_{A})=\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}](A))

which is a morphism of differential objects since each of the structural morphisms
\zeta
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{p}
are tangent natural transformations and thus compatible with the distributive law
\beta
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Given a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a differential structure
(\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
for
(G,\beta)
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
, the functor
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
together with the distributive law
\Lambda[\beta]
defines a lax tangent morphism:

\displaystyle\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]\colon(\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}],\Lambda[\beta])\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Proof.

In the previous discussion, we defined
\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
as the lax tangent morphism which picks out the differential object
(GA;\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
for each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
and whose distributive law is induced by
\beta
. In particular, the naturality of the structure morphisms
\zeta
,
\sigma
, and
\hat{p}
makes the functor
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
well-defined, the compatibility of the structure morphisms and
\beta
makes
\Lambda[\beta]
into a morphism of differential objects, and the compatibility of
\beta
with the tangent structures makes
\Lambda[G,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
into a lax tangent morphism.
∎

We can now prove the universal property of differential objects, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.8
.

The differential object
(\mathsf{U},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
of Proposition
3.5
is universal. Concretely, the induced strict tangent natural transformation

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}\colon[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]\to\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]

makes the functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{TNGCAT}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{[-\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]}\mathsf{TNGCAT}^{\mathsf{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DO}^{\mathsf{op}}}\mathsf{TNGCAT}^{\mathsf{op}}

which sends a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
to the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
, into a corepresentable functor. In particular,
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
is invertible.

Proof.

Consider a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and a lax tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, which sends each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential object
(FA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. We want to compare
(F,\alpha)
with
\Lambda[\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(F,\alpha)]
. Concretely,
\Lambda[\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(F,\alpha)]
sends an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential objects
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(F,\alpha)_{A}
. However, this corresponds to the differential object
(FA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. Given a morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\to(F^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
of
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]

\displaystyle\varphi_{A}\colon(FA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})\to(F^{\prime}A,\zeta^{\prime}_{A},\sigma^{\prime}_{A},\hat{p}^{\prime}_{A})

which coincides with the morphism
\Lambda[\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\varphi)]
, since:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\varphi)_{A}=FA\xrightarrow{\varphi}F^{\prime}A

Conversely, consider a differential object
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
of the Hom-tangent category
[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
. Therefore,
\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}]
sends each
A
to the differential object
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
. Thus,
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}])
coincides with
(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
. Similarly, for a given morphism
\varphi(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})\to(G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime};\zeta^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\hat{p}^{\prime})
of differential objects,
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}(\Lambda[\varphi])
coincides with
\varphi
.

Finally, it is not hard to see that the functor

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon\mathsf{DO}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\to[\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})]

extends to a strict tangent morphism, since both
\Lambda[\mathrm{T}^{\mathsf{DO}}(G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})]
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathrm{T}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\Lambda[G,\beta;\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p}])
send each
A\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the differential object

\displaystyle(\mathrm{T}GA,\mathrm{T}\zeta_{A},\mathrm{T}\sigma_{A},c_{A}\circ\mathrm{T}\hat{p}_{A})

Therefore,
\Lambda
inverts
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
.
∎

Theorem
3.8
establishes the correct universal property of the construction of differential objects. Thanks to this result, we can finally introduce the notion of differential objects in the formal context of tangentads.

Definition 3.9
.

A Cartesian tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
in a
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}

admits the construction of differential objects
if there exists a Cartesian tangentad
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
together with a (strict) tangent morphism
\mathsf{U}\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and a pointwise differential object
(\mathsf{U};\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
such that the induced tangent natural transformation
\Gamma_{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})}
of Lemma
3.6
is invertible. The pointwise differential object
(\mathsf{U};\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
is called the
universal pointwise differential object
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is called the
tangentad of differential objects
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Definition 3.10
.

A Cartesian
2
-category
{\mathbf{K}}

admits the construction of differential objects
provided that every Cartesian tangentad of
{\mathbf{K}}
admits the construction of differential objects.

We can now rephrase Theorem
3.8
as follows.

Corollary 3.11
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories admits the construction of differential objects and the tangentad of differential objects of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
\mathsf{CAT}
is the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

To ensure that Definitions
3.9
and
3.10
are well-posed, one requires the tangentad of differential objects of a given tangentad to be unique. The next proposition establishes that such a construction is defined uniquely up to a unique isomorphism.

Proposition 3.12
.

If a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
admits the construction of differential objects, the tangentad of differential objects
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is unique up to a unique isomorphism which extends to an isomorphism of the corresponding universal differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the universal property of the construction. In particular, suppose that
\mathsf{DO}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is another tangentad of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Using the universal property, the identity tangent morphism of
\mathsf{DO}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
must correspond to a morphism of pointwise differential objects in the Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]
, which, by the universal property of
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, corresponds to a tangent morphism
\mathsf{DO}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Dually, we can construct a tangent morphism
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DO}^{\prime}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and, by the universal property, these two tangent morphisms must invert each other.
∎

3.3
The formal structures of differential objects

In Section
3.1
, we recalled that (a) differential objects of a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
form a new tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
; (b) the assigment which sends a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is
2
-functorial.

In this section, we recover the
2
-functoriality of the construction of differential objects in the context of tangentads. For starters, notice that, in the context of tangentads, (a) is a built-in property of the definition of the tangentad of differential objects
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of a tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, since
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
is, by definition, a tangentad.

In the following,
{\mathbf{K}}
denotes a
2
-category which admits the construction of differential objects.

The functoriality of the construction of differential objects

As proved in Lemma
3.2
, there is a
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}\colon\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNGCAT}_{\cong}

which sends a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
to the Cartesian tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\otimes,\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

In this section, we show that the construction of differential objects is
2
-functorial in the
2
-category of tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
. Consider a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
between two Cartesian tangentads of
{\mathbf{K}}
which preserves finite products. It is not hard to prove that

\displaystyle[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]

is a strong tangent morphism which preserves finite products. Thus,
\mathsf{DO}
sends
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]
to a strong tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]}\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]\cong[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})]

where we used the universal property of differential objects to identify
\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]
with the Hom-tangent category
[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})]
. In particular, the universal differential object
\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}}
is sent by this tangent morphism to a tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})

Now, consider a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and define the tangent natural transformation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\varphi]\colon\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]\Rightarrow\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>G,\beta]

Thus, by evaluating such a transformation on the universal differential object and by employing the universal property of differential objects, we obtain a
2
-morphism:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\varphi)\colon\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow\mathsf{DO}(G,\beta)

Proposition 3.13
.

There is a
2
-functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}\colon\mathsf{CTNG}({\mathbf{K}})_{\cong}\to\mathsf{CTNG}({\mathbf{K}})_{\cong}

which sends a Cartesian tangentad
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
{\mathbf{K}}
to the tangentad of differential objects
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. In particular, for each strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, the tangent morphism
\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
is also strong.

Proof.

Consider a strong tangent morphism
(F,\alpha)\colon(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\to(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
between two Cartesian tangentads which preserves finite products. As previously discussed, let
\mathsf{DO}(F,\alpha)
the tangent morphism which correspond to the universal differential object
\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}}
via the functor

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]}\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime}]\cong[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})]

The underlying tangent morphism of
\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha](\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{\hat{P}})
is simply given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{U}}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\xrightarrow{(F,\alpha)}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

Given a
2
-morphism
\varphi\colon(F,\alpha)\Rightarrow(G,\beta)
,
\mathsf{DO}(\varphi)
is the
2
-morphism corresponding to the tangent natural transformation

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>\varphi]\colon\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>F,\alpha]\Rightarrow\mathsf{DO}[\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})\>|\>G,\beta]

evaluated at the universal differential object.
∎

3.4
Applications

In Section
2.1
, we listed some examples of tangentads. In this section, we compute the construction of differential objects for each of these examples. Unfortunately, the construction of differential objects cannot be obtained by means of PIE limits as done for vector fields in
[
15
, Theorem 4.8]
. The reason is that the universal property enjoyed by the differential projection of a differential object is a non-equational axiom, and PIE limits can only construct algebraic theories. Therefore, in this section, we follow a direct approach to construct differential objects for the different tangentads.

Tangent monads

In order to compute the construction of differential objects for tangent monads we need to first understand the notion of Cartesian tangent monads, which are Cartesian objects in the
2
-category of tangent monads.

A
Cartesian
tangent monad consists of a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
together with a tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
which preserves finite products. In general, we do not expect
(S,\alpha)
to lift to the categories of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
unless the distributive law
\alpha
is invertible. We call a
strong
Cartesian tangent monad a Cartesian monad whose underlying tangent morphism
(S,\alpha)
is strong.

Lemma 3.14
.

A strong Cartesian tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a Cartesian tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
lifts to a Cartesian tangent monad on the Cartesian tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Strong tangent morphisms send differential objects to differential objects. In particular,
S(A,\zeta,\sigma,\hat{p})
is the differential object
(SA,S\zeta,S\sigma,S\hat{p}\circ\alpha^{-1})
. Furthermore, by naturality and thanks to the compatibility with
\alpha
, the unit
\eta
and the multiplication
\mu
of
S
are morphisms of differential objects.
∎

Theorem 3.15
.

In the
2
-category
\mathsf{TNGMND}
of tangent monads, each strong Cartesian tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
on a Cartesian tangent category admits the construction of differential objects. Moreover, the tangentad of differential objects of
(S,\alpha)
is the Cartesian tangent monad
\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha)
on
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

In Lemma
3.14
, we already proved that
\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha)
defines a tangent monad on the tangent category
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
of differential objects of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Now, we want to prove that
(\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T}),\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha))
represents the tangentad of differential objects of the tangent monad
(S,\alpha)
. Consider a tangent category
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
equipped with a tangent monad
(S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
. A differential object in the Hom-tangent category
[(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime};S^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\>|\>(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T};S,\alpha)]
is a lax tangent morphism
(G,\beta)\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
equipped with a distributive law
\tau\colon S\circ G\to G\circ S^{\prime}
, compatible with the monad structures and the distributive laws
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
. Furthermore,
(G,\beta;\tau)
comes equipped with tangent natural transformations

\displaystyle\zeta_{A}\colon\ast\to GA

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}\colon GA\times GA\to GA

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{A}\colon\mathrm{T}GA\to GA

which make each
GA
into a differential object of
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Moreover,
\zeta
,
\sigma
,
\hat{p}
must also be natural transformations of monads, that is, they must be compatible with
\tau
. Thus, the lax tangent morphism

\displaystyle(H,\gamma)\colon

\displaystyle(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends each
A\in(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
to
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
comes equipped with a distributive law
\theta

\displaystyle\theta_{A}\colon\mathsf{DO}(S,\alpha)(HA)=(SGA,S\zeta_{A},S\sigma_{A},\alpha^{-1}\circ S\hat{p}_{A})\xrightarrow{\gamma_{A}}(GS^{\prime}A,GS^{\prime}\zeta_{A},GS^{\prime}\sigma_{A},{\alpha^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ GS^{\prime}\hat{p}_{A})=H(S^{\prime}A)

which makes
(H,\gamma)
into a morphisms of tangent monads. We let the reader prove that
(H,\gamma;\tau)
is the unique such morphism.
∎

Tangent fibrations

In this section, we consider the construction of differential objects for tangent fibrations. First, let us unwrap the definition of a Cartesian tangent fibration, that is, a Cartesian object in the
2
-category of tangent fibrations.

A
Cartesian
tangent fibration consists of a tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between two Cartesian tangent categories
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
whose underlying functor strictly preserves finite products, that is,
\Pi
sends the terminal object of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the (chosen) terminal object of
\mathbb{X}
and sends each Cartesian product
E\times F
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
to the (chosen) product
\Pi(E)\times\Pi(F)
of
\mathbb{X}
. Moreover, for each pair of morphisms
f\colon A\to C
and
g\colon B\to D
of
\mathbb{X}
, the Cartesian product

\displaystyle\varphi_{f}\times\varphi_{g}\colon f^{\ast}E\times g^{\ast}F\to E\times F

of the (cloven) Cartesian lifts
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
and
\varphi_{g}\colon g^{\ast}F\to F
is the (cloven) Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f\times g}
of
f\times g
onto
E\times F
.

Lemma 3.16
.

Consider a (cloven) Cartesian tangent fibration
\Pi\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
between two Cartesian tangent categories. The strict tangent morphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

which sends a differential object
(E,\zeta_{E},\sigma_{E},\hat{p}_{E})
to the differential object
(\Pi(E),\Pi(\zeta_{E}),\Pi(\sigma_{E}),\Pi(\hat{p}_{E}))
is a (cloven) Cartesian tangent fibration.

Proof.

Consider a (linear) morphism
f\colon(A,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})\to(B,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})
of differential objects in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and let
(E,\zeta_{E},\sigma_{E},\hat{p}_{E})
be a differential object of
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
such that
\Pi(E,\zeta_{E},\sigma_{E},\hat{p}_{E})=(B,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})
. Using the universal property of the Cartesian lift
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
we can define the morphisms

\displaystyle f^{\ast}\zeta_{E}\colon\ast\to f^{\ast}E

\displaystyle f^{\sigma}_{E}\colon f^{\ast}E\times f^{\ast}E\to f^{\ast}E

\displaystyle f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E\to f^{\ast}E

uniquely defined as the lifts of the structure morphisms of the differential object
A
which commute with
\varphi_{f}
is the obvious way. Since
f^{\ast}\colon\Pi^{-1}(B)\to\Pi^{-1}(A)
is functorial, the equational axioms which make
f^{\ast}E\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(f^{\ast}E,f^{\ast}\zeta_{E},f^{\ast}\sigma_{E},f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E})
into a differential object hold. Thus, we only need to prove the universal property of
f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}
. Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{E}}

By the universal property of
\hat{p}_{E}
, there exists a unique morphism
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{E}\colon X\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{E}}

However, this diagram lifts the diagram in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Pi(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Pi(\alpha),\Pi(\beta)\rangle_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{B}}

Thus, by the universal property of
\hat{p}_{A}
and by the universal property of
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}
, there exists a unique morphism
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{f^{\ast}E}\colon X\to\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E
such that
\Pi(\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{f^{\ast}E})=\langle\Pi(\alpha),\Pi(\beta)\rangle_{A}\colon\Pi(X)\to\mathrm{T}A
, making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{E}}

Thus,
f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}
is universal. We leave it to the reader to show that
\varphi_{f}
is Cartesian in
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
.
∎

Theorem 3.17
.

The
2
-category
\mathsf{FIB}
of (cloven) fibrations admits the construction of differential objects. In particular, the tangentad of differential objects of a (cloven) Cartesian tangent fibration
\Pi
is the Cartesian tangent fibration
\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)\colon\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Consider a tangent fibration
\Pi_{\bullet}\colon(\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and a morphism
(G,\beta;G^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\colon\Pi_{\bullet}\to\Pi
of tangent fibrations, together with a differential object structure in the Hom-tangent category
[\Pi_{\bullet},\Pi]
. Concretely, this consists of a list of natural transformations

\displaystyle\zeta_{A}

\displaystyle\colon\ast\to GA

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}_{E}

\displaystyle\colon\ast\to G^{\prime}E

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}

\displaystyle\colon GA\times GA\to GA

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}_{E}

\displaystyle\colon G^{\prime}E\times G^{\prime}E\to G^{\prime}E

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{A}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{T}GA\to GA

\displaystyle\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E}

\displaystyle\colon\mathrm{T}^{\prime}G^{\prime}E\to G^{\prime}E

for each
A\in(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and
E\in(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})
, such that
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
and
(G^{\prime}E,\zeta_{E}^{\prime},\sigma_{E}^{\prime},\hat{p}_{E}^{\prime})
are differential objects in
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, respectively and satisfying the following equations:

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)}=\Pi(\zeta^{\prime}_{E})

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)}=\Pi(\sigma^{\prime}_{E})

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)}=\Pi(\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E})

for every
E\in(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. In particular, for each
E\in(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)(G^{\prime}E,\zeta^{\prime}_{E},\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E})=(\Pi(G^{\prime}E),\Pi(\zeta^{\prime}_{E}),\Pi(\sigma^{\prime}_{E}),\Pi(\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E})=(G\Pi_{\bullet}(E),\zeta_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)},\sigma_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)},\hat{p}_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)})

Define the following tangent morphisms:

\displaystyle(H,\gamma)

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})

\displaystyle(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})

which send each
A\in(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and each
E\in(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet}^{\prime})
to
(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})
and
(G^{\prime}E,\zeta^{\prime}_{E},\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E})
, respectively. Thus:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)(H^{\prime}(E))=\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)(G^{\prime}E,\zeta^{\prime}_{E},\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\hat{p}^{\prime}_{E})=(G\Pi_{\bullet}(E),\zeta_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)},\sigma_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)},\hat{p}_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)})=H^{\prime}_{\Pi_{\bullet}(E)}

In particular,
(H,\gamma)
and
(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
commute with the tangent fibrations
\Pi_{\bullet}
and
\mathsf{DO}(\Pi)
. To prove that
(H,\gamma;H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
is a morphism of tangent fibrations, we need to show that
(H,H^{\prime})
preserves Cartesian lifts. Consider a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of
(\mathbb{X}_{\bullet},\mathbb{T}_{\bullet})
and let
\varphi_{f}\colon f^{\ast}E\to E
be a Cartesian lift of
f
along
\Pi_{\bullet}
. Since
(G,G^{\prime})\colon\Pi_{\bullet}\to\Pi
is a morphism of fibrations,
G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}\colon G^{\prime}F^{\ast}E\to G^{\prime}E
is a Cartesian lift of
Gf\colon GA\to GB
. Furthermore, by the naturality of the structure morphisms
(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})
,
(\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
, and
(\hat{p},\hat{p}^{\prime})
,
H^{\prime}\varphi_{f}=G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}\colon H^{\prime}f^{\ast}E\to H^{\prime}E
is a morphism of differential objects. We need to prove that
H^{\prime}\varphi_{f}=G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}
is Cartesian in
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
. Consider a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(GC,\zeta_{C},\sigma_{C},\hat{p}_{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(GA,\zeta_{A},\sigma_{A},\hat{p}_{A})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(GB,\zeta_{B},\sigma_{B},\hat{p}_{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hf}

in
\mathsf{DO}(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
. Since
G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}
is Cartesian in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
, there exists a unique morphism
\xi_{h}\colon G^{\prime}f^{\ast}E\to G^{\prime}E
in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{T}^{\prime})
such that
\Pi(\xi_{h})=h
and making the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{\ast}G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}

We want to prove that
\xi_{h}
is a morphism of differential objects. For the sake of brevity, we show how to prove the compatibility between
\xi_{h}
and the differential projections and leave it to the reader to apply the same technique for the other compatibilities. Consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}g^{\ast}G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{\ast}G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}G^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}f^{\ast}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}\hat{p}_{G^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}\xi_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{p}_{G^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\prime}G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}f^{\ast}\hat{p}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}}

The right and the left triangles commute since
G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}\circ\xi_{h}=\varphi_{g}
and so do the top and bottom parallelogram-shaped diagrams since
G^{\prime}\varphi_{f}
and
\varphi_{g}
are morphisms of differential objects. Therefore:
